ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U1	The second reading of the draft law amending the Order of Defence Forces Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic, begins. I would like to ask my colleague Mati Raidma, Chairman of the National Defence Committee, to serve as a waitress.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U2	Dear Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, The National Defence Committee discussed the draft law amending the Order of Defence Forces Act 256 before the second reading at its meeting on 5 November and decided by consensus to propose to the Board of the Riigikogu that the draft law be included for the second reading on the agenda of the current sitting of the Riigikogu and that the speaker be appointed as rapporteur. The only amendment to the draft was tabled by the National Defence Committee as the lead committee, approved by consensus and supported by the initiator of the draft amendment. The amendment was tabled on the recommendation of Aivar Engel, Head of the Adviser-Secretariat. The National Defence Committee, when proposing the amendment, was primarily based on the technical clarity of the norm and on the fact that a change in the title of the draft should be proposed as an amendment rather than as a technical amendment to the norm. In cooperation with the senior editor of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu, the draft also went through a linguistic proofreading, where the most important thing is the knowledge (if anyone listens to me) that from 1 April next year the word 'Defence Forces' will be used with a capital letter. At its meeting on 5 November, the National Defence Committee decided by consensus to approve the text of the second reading of the draft, the table of amendments and to submit an explanatory memorandum for the second reading. The National Defence Committee also decided by consensus to propose to the Riigikogu to stop the second reading of bill 256. Thank you!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U3	Thank you very much, colleague. You have questions. Please, colleague Heimar Lenk!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U4	Thank you, dear waitress. Why don't you explain why suddenly the word "guards" started to be written with a capital letter when it was written with a small letter?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U5	Please, colleague Marika Tuus-Laul!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U6	Thank you. It was a common name before, but now a political decision was made that the Defence Forces were one. The Defence Forces are an institution with its own structural units. In the case of a single institution, however, it is customary to start with a large star. But this amendment will be in accordance with the law, which will be in force in spring next year.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U7	Thank you. Honourable waitress! If we look at this draft explanatory memorandum, it says that the defence forces will be used only at extreme need. However, it has not been explained what has actually been meant by this need. As a result, we would like to know what defines the extreme need for which the right to use the armed forces arises.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U8	Thank you. This is an international principle whereby military forces, or armed forces, are used in the event of all other possibilities being exhausted. The extreme need does not mean armaments, but also involves the involvement of technical means, logistical support, etc. But it's just being used in the last order, that's all.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U9	Please, colleague Mikhail Stalnukhin!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U10	Thank you, Madam President. Dear waitress, These long discussions will help you get new knowledge, surf the Internet and read any interesting information. For example, it became clear that there are three ways to use the armed forces to assist the police. Defence forces are basically used during peacetimes when there are disturbances and during wartimes when the police cannot cope or their work is associated with serious difficulties. Also, as a police force, dictatorships use it as a rule. If such a law comes, it would be interesting to know what we are preparing for: unrest, war or dictatorship.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U11	Thank you. I recommend surfing domestic sites. The use of domestic defence forces is regulated by the Emergency Act and by the Emergency Act, those blows where the defence forces can be used together with the right to use their forces. The current amendment, I drew down, concerns the involvement of the armed forces without the use of force, i.e. without the right to use direct coercion, which is a completely different matter.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U19	Thank you, Madam President. Honourable waitress! My question relates to a specific person, a conscript, if that is the way to put it. If a conscript is engaged in additional duties in accordance with the cases set out in the draft and his training period is therefore reduced, how will it be ensured that the level of training of a military officer is not affected by it? The solution to this issue needs to be known in advance. How do you comment on that?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U20	Thank you. It is a great dilemma, and it has been discussed for years, even though life has shaken its place. On the one hand, defence forces are rarely used and attempts are being made to take members of the Defence Forces away from their daily service in a very short period of time. On the other hand, it is a positive signal to civil society that the defence forces will be involved in solving real problems. I'm not talking about the use of force here, I'm talking about rescue operations and stuff like that. So this balance exists and all parties know, appreciate and accept it.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U24	Please, Mr Viktor Vassilyev!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U25	Thank you, Madam President. Good waitress! Please tell me, as a result of which a circle of persons has been defined to whom the military police will provide protection and whether this circle may be extended under any exceptional circumstances. And if this circle can be extended, please specify to whom this right could be extended and what the legal basis would be for it to be associated with its law.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U26	Thank you. When we talk about foreign citizens and high-ranking officers, there is a United Nations document of 14 December 1973 that obliges us to guarantee the security of these persons. As regards the length of this list, the possible extension, the Minister of Defence is indeed entitled to appoint a circle of persons to whom this protection may be extended for a certain period of time. An example of this is that the National Defence Commission went on a mission in Afghanistan and we were secured by the close defence team of the Estonian military police, who serves in Afghanistan. So the members of the National Defence Commission have also used this service.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U45	Please, Mr Kalla, the second question.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U46	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office. Dear waitress, the chairman of the committee! I had a question about the one and only amendment. This amendment is made in relation to the title and is quite substantive, practically adding another amendment to the law. In the beginning there was a "law on the organisation of the Defence Forces," and now there was a law amending the law on the implementation of the Defence Forces Service Act. This is an amendment made by the Commission. Can you explain why this was not the title of the original draft submitted by the Ministry? Or did the substance of the bill change so much that they were forced to change the title so seriously, to supplement it with another law?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U47	Thank you. There are many new laws behind this that we have been able to adopt here. For example, the Civil Service Act has lost the term 'public servant', and so we have also developed the need to make improvements in national defence laws, i.e. to introduce the term'service place' instead of a civil servant. It does not contain a very large political decision, but we considered it reasonable to clarify the title in terms of clarity. We did not see it as a risk that this would be a very substantive amendment to another law. This, in turn, is due to an amendment to the Law on the organisation of the Defence Forces in one section, where we also adjust terminology in the light of the new Public Service Act. These laws should become linguistically uniform in conjunction. If you want very detailed information, you can contact Aivar Engel, the head of our committee's secretariat. He's probably better able to surf this concept in the world than I am.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U48	Please, Mr Aivar Riisalu!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U49	Thank you, good manager. A good committee chairman, a good colleague. Whenever it comes to the use of the armed forces in the exercise of their civil duties, or police tasks, with the concordance that this without the right to use the force, the emotion immediately arises, taking into account the recent history of society, that now the military will be used against its people, or probably not the end of what this law means. Would you be so kind as to list (our verbatim is certainly quite interesting material in this matter for the public and the press) the police tasks that the defence forces are actually carrying out within the framework of this law, so that we can make sure that there is obviously no rioting?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U50	Thank you. Again, these tasks are divided into three laws when we talk about the use of force. I will rather read out the points in the Emergency Act, which show that issues that raise questions are regulated in another law. Functions such as traffic management during emergency situations and security in the emergency area, and all related to the prevention and prevention of terrorist offences, action to combat the intrusion into the state or local government's office, action against attacks aimed at the life and health of an internationally protected person, action against areas with diplomatic immunity, the building and space invasion, the prevention or prevention of damage to objects at high risk of attack, the temporary restriction or suspension of the crossing of state borders, all of which are regulated by § 31 of the Emergency Act. It also says that this can only be decided by order with the consent of the President of the Republic. This point in the draft regulation regulates activities which do not involve force or direct coercion, by means of the examples given above. These activities are mainly based on the use of specific techniques used by the Defence Forces, such as floating equipment, helicopters and armored vehicles (not in direct contact with people or in the use of force), rather than logistical tasks.

It also includes paragraph 1 (1) - rescue and emergency work. The procedure for the use of the Defence Forces for the performance of these tasks shall be established by the Government of the Republic by means of a regulation which also describes the arrangements for bearing the costs.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U78	Please, Mr Aivar Riisalu, the second question!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U79	Thank you, President-in-Office. Good colleague! Basically, we have had a very busy last year in the National Defence Committee. We have dealt with the Defence Service Act, we currently have a full text of the Defence League Act. This specific law, the Act amending the Law on the Organisation of the Defence Forces and the Act on the Implementation of the Defence Forces Service Act, has somehow been broken apart from our 'fundamental laws'. Where, at the same time, did it become necessary to review these civil functions relatively quickly in the law? I understand the subject of the Special Operations Group very well, but it is precisely a matter of emphasising the civilian side separately, and so suddenly.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U80	Thank you. One of the reasons for this need is that the Police and Border Guard Board also has functions that need to be performed without the use of force, as I have just listed above (rescue and search), as border guards and police have joined. These tasks were not described in the Rescue Act and were now to be introduced into the Law on the Order of the Defence Forces. These were mentioned in the Police and Border Guard Act, but it was also considered appropriate to supplement the law on the organisation of the defence forces in the field of defence, i.e. to provide the defence forces with the sections of their law, on which they can support the Police and Border Guard Board (the subject of this draft) or the Rescue Board (the subject of the Emergency Act).
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U81	Esther Tuiksoo, please!

These three laws are, in the Commission's view, now exhaustively governing the rules governing the use of defence forces.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U109	Ladies and gentlemen, It is a delightful fact that so much passion, substantive questions and substantive answers come with the draft. As you can see on the screen, there are a lot of people besides me who want to talk. By quoting the words of the classics, or to say the words of Rein Lang, I think we are dealing with a very good draft or, if not very good, at least a rather good draft. In particular, if this draft becomes a law, certain inconsistencies that have existed so far will be eliminated. After all, we've all seen in the city pictures the military police machines in front of and behind the column of high DOD guest cars. According to the current legislation, the Defence Forces can only be used to assist the police in certain cases by a decision of the Government of the Republic, subject to the consent of the President of the Republic. It was not directly concerned with ensuring the security of foreign guests, but finally the rights of the military police in the role of foreign visitors as a security guard are regulated by law and legally clear. Of course, it is regrettable that when the law on the organisation of the Defence Forces was completed not long ago in the Ministry of Defence, someone in the ministry missed such an important moment in connection with the duties of the military police. But in any case, it's better late than never, and it's nice that the role of the military police can finally be properly established in legal terms. This correction will certainly benefit the military police, who will be given a clear legal framework and certainty to carry out their duties. The military police are also worth it because they have so far shown themselves to be the best. I can't recall any incidents involving the work of military police officers either in the security of foreign guests or in other tasks. There is also reason to give good credit to Estonian military police and to thank the members of the National Defence Committee who have visited foreign visits in crisis areas. While in Estonia military police officers offer protection to foreign visitors arriving in our country, for example, during the last visit to Afghanistan, members of the National Defence Commission were secured by our own military police officers. The same draft gives the Defence Forces the right to participate in police tasks and I would stress that this is without the right to exercise force. Although there are still no legal cases where the police can turn to the Defence Forces to ask for assistance, the practice shows that such situations have actually occurred. Frequently, there are situations where the Defence Forces have the technology that the police would need to perform a task. There are also suitably trained people who can walk around with this technique. This draft provides for a more flexible use of defence forces in such cases. As the involvement of the Defence Forces in the exercise of police functions is provided for without the right to exercise force, the decision to provide assistance at a high political level is no longer necessary. The decision to grant the aid may be taken by the defence forces themselves when assessing their own resources. This moment is very important, as the main task of the Defence Forces, which is to ensure national security, must certainly not be affected in connection with the performance of remote tasks. It is expected that the Defence Forces themselves will be best placed to assess their own resources and decide whether they will be able to participate in the ancillary task without compromising national defence capabilities. It is certainly important to note that the defence forces are used only for extreme needs. For example, in a situation where, for whatever reason, the police cannot carry out their task on time and there is no other way to carry out the task. This innovation will certainly lead to a reduction in public spending. In the case of a small country, it would indeed be an excessive luxury to duplicate a fleet of machinery which may not be needed very often. Certainly, a more flexible and operational response from now on will contribute to increasing the security of all of us in the use of military technology and human power in specific cases. However, it must certainly be borne in mind here that the State must reimburse the defence forces for the additional costs incurred in carrying out their duties. The content of the amendment contained in the last draft is that instead of the Estonian contingent of the Cyber Defence Competence Centre in the structure of the staff and communication battalion, a new structure unit of the Defence Forces will be created directly under the command of the Commander of the Defence Forces, the Estonian contingent of the NATO Centre for Cyber Defence Cooperation, which will make the structure of the Defence Forces simpler and more transparent. The establishment of a new structural unit under the direct command of the Commander of the Defence Forces will ensure the shortest possible, clear and operational chain of command and effective operational supervision. The planning of the salary and mission expenses of the Estonian contingent will take place in a more transparent and targeted way. As stated in the explanatory memorandum, placing Estonia's contingent under the direct command of the Commander of the Defence Forces would be a sensible step, given the status of the Centre as an international military headquarters and the political visibility it entails. It is not common for drafts coming from the government to benefit the Estonian people. However, it is more common that the drafts coming from the offices of ministers to the Riigikogu are accompanied by price increases, uncertainty and ignorance for people. Fortunately, however, the government has had a common sense and a sense of responsibility, at least in the field of defence, where, as you know, it is simply not enough to pick on everyone involved. The Riigikogu National Defence Committee also added its own addition to the draft, with which it became even better. So I think it is worth supporting this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U123	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I was moved by a remote control to come here with a few motives. I'm fascinated by the cyber defence issues on this bill. This is the kind of part of the defence force's activities where all contributions are placed on intelligence, education and knowledge. I like cyber defence and the emphasis on this wisdom especially because it has captured the traditional traditions typical of our area. I am not even going to talk about the Estonian War of Independence. You have all heard about this, and also about how brave Estonian officers were and how well educated they were, compared to some other countries. After all, the officer's staff was mainly composed of school teachers in the municipality. This wisdom has been a major force in our field of warfare for centuries. I think maybe a few of you have heard that already during the Northern War, thanks to their abilities, the first Estonian officers grew up. One of the most famous was Thomas of Jöri, who ascended to be the lieutenant of the Oberst lieutenant, a lieutenant Colonel of ours. He was indeed a very wise and educational man. After returning from the prisoner of war, he worked with his expense and letters at the Piuketsa Manor in Türi Parish for the peasants of the school, further developed by education. The main time, however, when Estonia was known for its wise warriors over the empire was the 19th century. Perhaps it is surprising for some to know that in the 19th century, the University of Tartu, which was not a military educational institution, was able to train more than 100 men with general banners. Some military schools didn't even get such results. I'm sure you know General Field Marshal von Berg, but there were also a lot of distinguished men you probably haven't heard from. All these men were the ones who emerged in military warfare because of extraordinary wisdom and knowledge. More known name could be Lieutenant General von Helmersen, who was also known in the scientific world, grew up as an academician and was the most influential men in the Russian state, including in the field of intelligence. Perhaps less has been heard of such men, or has not been heard of them at all, like General von Goens, who has taken over the land of the Kazakhs, who has gone into the history of war. He was not at all a physicist or a geographer or a representative of one of these harsher disciplines, but a philologist. As a result, he was a very good commander because he knew the languages of the Orient. This brought him many successes, even in the sense of finding resources. These were not discovered at the time, but had to speak the local language, put a little pressure on them, and then it was possible to find the mineral. Very famous were those men in the Russian army who had studied under the control of a well-known astronomer Struve, including his own sons who made outstanding careers. It was a miracle of science at that time because they had studied astronomy, but they didn't do astronomy anywhere in the corner of the empire. Thanks to them, the empire prepared the necessary topographical maps, not only for the areas that belonged to the empire, but also for the areas that did not yet belong to the empire, but soon thereafter. For example, the well - known Peter von Helmersen charted Amurland when it was still Chinese. Very wise were those graduates of the University of Tartu who, thanks to their wisdom, arrived... Three more minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U124	Please, three minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U125	There were still men with smart generals or admiral banners. Smaller men may not have known that language. ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U144	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, This draft 281 was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 27 September 2012 by the Constitutional Commission. The Commission discussed the draft before its first reading in plenary session on 9 October. The first reading of the draft was held in plenary on 17 October. a and the first reading was completed. The deadline for amendments was set at 6 p.m. on 31 October. No amendments were received within the deadline. On 31 October, a referral to the Estonian Judicial Association arrived on the draft. In preparation for the second reading of the draft, which took place on 5 November and 12 November, Vice Chancellor Marko Aavik of the Ministry of Justice and Ülle Pulk, Chairman of the Board of the Association of Estonian Judicial Aids, took part as guests. The Chairman of the Executive Board of the Association of Estonian Judicial Assistants presented to the Commission the proposals they made, according to which the six-month probationary period should not apply to the post of Advocate General when candidates for the post of Advocate General are adjourned, as the assistance of judges has already passed the preparatory service, etc. On behalf of the Ministry of Justice, the association commented on the proposal and outlined the position of the Ministry of Justice, Marko Aavik. According to that opinion, the judge's assistants cannot be exempted from the application of the probationary period, since the post of Advocate General includes different functions compared to what the judges' assistants are currently doing. The members of the Commission listened to the views of the parties and asked for further questions. The majority of the members of the Commission considered that it was not justified to create preferential conditions for the assistance of judges who stand as Advocates-General compared to other candidates. They should therefore be subject to the probationary period provided for in the draft. The same view was taken by several leading Estonian judges. The Commission also discussed some inconsistencies between the draft text and the explanatory memorandum. It has now been removed and the draft has been submitted to second reading.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U177	It is a question of whether we need to anticipate all kinds of activities that he does and does not do. The most important thing about this draft is that the Advocate General works under the supervision and direction of a judge. It's important. He certainly does not make independent procedural decisions, that is out of the question. There is no compromise in this regard, the judge decides. The Advocate General, under the guidance of the Judge, prepares the material needed for the judge to decide, and likewise he prepares, for example, draft judgments or orders and other procedural documents. The courts produce numerous procedural documents. In order not to spend the time of the judge in this work, the Advocate General will help him. This is a matter of cooperation between the two. It cannot be regulated in very detailed terms. Clearly, according to the doctrine of justice, decisions are made by a judge and not by an Advocate General.

In addition, in the final stage, the opinions concerned whether the assistance of the judge should be exempted from the generally prescribed six-month probationary period. We didn't find it necessary, and the judges didn't think it was necessary. The other part of the question was whether the Advocate General would ease the work of the Judge. I'm sure it will. The judges long await the creation of such a post in order to have reliable and qualified people there, to reduce the technical, routine work of the judges and to commit more to substantive review and decision-making.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U184	Mikhail Stalnukhin, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U185	Thank you, Mr President. Dear waitress, One of the objectives of the draft act and the reason for the creation of the Advocate General's posts is the need to ensure a reasonable period of procedure and to improve the quality of justice. This means that the explanatory memorandum assumes that there are serious problems with these two issues. As former Chairman of the Supreme Court and former judge of the European Court of Human Rights, how do you assess the quality of Estonian justice and whether the trial in Estonia takes place within a reasonable time?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U186	Thank you! Looking at this background, how quickly justice takes place in other European countries, such as Italy, Estonia looks very good. But we would always like to get faster and faster and better quality. However, on average, in my opinion, the speed of Estonian administration of justice is not condemnable, it cannot be said that there is something wrong with it, it is happening in a very normal way. What gives rise to difficulties is that lawyers often pick up not the content of the matter, but the way in which procedural documents are prepared, whether there are contradictions or weaknesses inside. When processual documents are technically poorly prepared, it is very easy for lawyers to comply with it, to challenge any procedural and substantive steps. The creation of an Advocate General's office will improve the quality of preparation of procedural documents and will also reduce the possibility of challenging procedural steps and thus delaying processes or creating empty but inevitably related disputes. Thus, the Advocate General should contribute to increasing speed and quality, as the level of preparation of procedural documents should presumably increase and thus speed up the judicial process.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U188	Dear Vice-Chairman, Ladies and gentlemen, I will start with the so-called positive side of this bill and the approval of the bill. Personally, I think that the position of Advocate General with the judges and in the courts is necessary, because the explanatory memorandum to the draft does indeed set out this issue, to which Mr Stalnukhin also pointed out. This concerns both the quality of justice in Estonian courts and the reasonable period of proceedings. I will bring you some information about the time of proceedings, as well as the number of judges, which will undoubtedly determine the time of proceedings. In 2010 there were 244 judges in Estonian courts and 13 of them had not been filled. In 2011, 242 judges had posts, 18 outstanding. This year there are 242 judges and 21 are not filled. In the last three years, as you can see, the number of vacant judges has increased steadily: from 13 to 21 in three years. However, the trend is that this number is increasing. When it comes to proceedings, for example in civil cases at first instance, the duration of proceedings is 164 days, 346 days in general proceedings, 142 days in administrative cases, 54 days in criminal cases at second instance, 167 days in civil cases and 191 days on average in administrative cases. If you look at the coalition agreement that the Reform Party and the Fatherland and Res Publica Union have concluded, there is a point in that agreement according to which the period of proceedings is to be brought to 100 days. When I read you these figures, as they are today, there is no doubt that there is a need to shorten the period of proceedings, and I think that the creation of an Advocate General's post should certainly help that. Speaking of this bill, as I said on my question, the bill was much more extensive than what we have on the table today when the Ministry came up with the so-called long version. In addition to the creation of the Advocate General's office, the draft also referred to the preparation of judges, and there was a separate position such as a senior court attorney. Unfortunately, I have the impression that this long draft was not agreed with the parties, whether it be the courts or the court administration council, or within the Ministry of Justice itself. The Chancellor of Justice's opinion was on almost ten pages. The ministry and the minister decided to come up with this shorter version, go the easy way of resisting and simply create the position of Advocate General. On the budget line, EUR 2.5 million has already been earmarked for this. The error of the short version of the draft proposal may indeed be the one on which there were a number of questions and to which the Chancellor of Justice also refers in his position: the competence of the Advocate General is defined in a relatively general way in the current draft and would require a little more dissertation. At the same time, I agree that this really is a very new position in the Estonian judicial system, and perhaps in time it will become clear what tasks the Advocate General will have in particular. If necessary, the law may also be further specified or supplemented and this competence may be rewritten or extended or restricted. I would also like to talk about the referral of the Association of Assistant Judges, which was also discussed by the Chairman of the Commission. We did try in the Commission, especially the Minister, to give assurance to the Association of Assistant Judges and the Assistant Judges that these posts would not be lost. Can I have three more minutes?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U189	Three minutes in case it wasn't added to start. Okay, three more minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U190	Thank you. The Association of Assistant Judges has also called on the Commission to obtain further assurance that their position will not be lost. The representative of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs explained to the Commission that § 294 (2) did not apply in practice and that the initiator of the draft was originally intended to amend § 301. ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U300	The second reading of the draft law 288 on the amendment of the Law on the Protection of Animals initiated by the Government of the Republic and the related amendment of other laws begins. I would like to ask my colleague Kalvi Tõud, Chairman of the Committee on Rural Affairs, for a presentation.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U301	Good Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, I am here before you to amend the Law on the Protection of Animals and, in connection with it, to amend the Law on the Amendment of Other Laws 288. This draft law we dealt with in this Chamber at first reading in the s. On 17 October, and then Minister of Agriculture Helir-Valdor Seeder gave a long and comprehensive overview of it. There is also an explanatory statement on the bill, which I'm sure you've been familiar with, so you know what the bill is made up of. I will focus on the amendments in particular here today at second reading. In the Commission, we discussed this draft law on Monday, 5 November. a. It must be said that the members of the Riigikogu, the political groups and other committees did not submit amendments within the deadline. However, the Rural Affairs Committee itself, in cooperation with both the Ministry and the Estonian Breeders' Association, formulated five amendments. I'll stop them. The first amendment. Paragraph 1 (6) of the draft law amends Paragraph 13 (1) and reads as follows: 'A poultry, rabbit and rabbit kept as an agricultural animal, which are slaughtered outside the slaughterhouse for their own use, must be stunned before slaughter and, if necessary, restricted before stunning. ' The bill that was in this hall at first reading was not a rabbit among these animals. Since it has become clear that we do also have the farms where the rabbit is kept as an agricultural animal, it was appropriate to include the rabbit here. It must be said that this amendment found consensus in the Commission. The second amendment amends the wording of § 13 (7). It was also a matter of first reading in this Chamber; the question was raised by the order of words. The new wording is 'good practice of killing and execution', before it was 'good practice of killing and execution'. In our opinion, it is not appropriate in law to say 'good practice of killing and execution'. Amendment 2 therefore formulates this paragraph in the draft text as follows: 'The Agency for Veterinary and Food publishes on its website and provides the European Commission with guidance on good practice in killing and killing, developed in accordance with Article 13 of Council Regulation (EC) No 1099/2009 and approved by the Agency. ' This amendment, too, was supported by a consensus by the lead committee. Proposal No 3 concerns subsection 24 of § 1 of the draft law, which relates to § 44 (6) of the Act. It shall be worded as follows: 'The holder shall submit to the Ministry of Agriculture, by 1 February, a record of each animal test carried out or under way in the previous year. ' The draft, which was in this Chamber at first reading, had no regulation or obligation to submit a protocol to the Ministry of Agriculture by 1 February on an ongoing animal test. So the word 'ongoing' is what is included in the draft law with the amendment. In order to characterise Amendment 4, it can be said that it brings the draft law more closely into line with the Euro Directive. The fifth amendment is motivated by a letter from the Estonian Association of Breeders, where the current Law on Breeding of Farm Animals requires the disclosure of all the information on breeding animals entered in the breeding book and hybrid breeding pigs entered in the breeding register in accordance with the Personal Data Protection Act. However, given the large number of breeding animals, in particular hybrid breeding pigs and cattle, the obligation to disclose these data is not reasonable or necessary for development purposes. From the point of view of the breeding of all animal species, it is important to disclose only the pedigree and performance data of male animals. Therefore, with this legislative amendment, we commit that the breed society must publish on the website at least the pedigree data of the male animals entered in the breeding book in accordance with the Data Protection Act, because the identity of the owners must be included in these data. All five amendments were supported by consensus by the Commission. The Commission also adopted, at its meeting on 5 November, such consensual decisions: to put a draft law on 288 plenary agendas. By 14 November, propose that the second reading of bill 288 be discontinued and, when the second reading is completed, send the draft to the third reading on 21 November 2012. These proposals were also made by consensus. We also made a decision in the Commission that, when the second reading of the draft law is closed, the proposal is to adopt the draft law as 288. This was not decided by consensus: 5 members of the Commission were in favour, 1 against and 2 abstentions. I look forward to hearing your questions. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U307	Thank you, my good colleague. It must be said that we did not discuss these issues so specifically at the committee meeting. However, it is not quite the case that all animals can be used for scientific purposes for animal testing. The use of free-living animals, stray animals, endangered animals and also non-human primates is generally prohibited in animal testing. That's what the law says. They may only be used in special cases where the purpose of the test cannot be achieved by other animal. All these exceptions are set out in §§ 36, 37 and 371 of the Animal Protection Act, on page 5 of the Bill. An animal test shall also be prohibited where the purpose of the test can be achieved by other methods or the test relates to the demonstration of compliance with the health requirements of cosmetic products or their constituents. So it is not quite the case that all animals can be used for animal testing. I thought you said that.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U308	Please, colleague Heimar Lenk!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U309	Good waitress! The committee was talking about it, but maybe it would be good to know in the big hall. Would you mind telling me why these improvements are being made to this law right now? Does this come from European requirements, or has life itself been proposing that the law should be amended in this way? Would you just talk about it in popular form?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U310	Thank you. The law has two objectives. Indeed, one comes from the Euro Directive, which is intended to regulate experimental animals. It can be said that it is precisely because of the European and global animal welfare objectives that the slaughter is also regulated for religious purposes. We know that in a very large number of European countries this matter is very precisely regulated. There are also countries in Europe where there are no exceptions to the slaughter of farm animals for religious purposes. Since this issue is highly controversial, we must, on the one hand, worry about animal welfare and, on the other hand, also take into account the wishes of different communities, it is imperative that this very complicated matter is regulated at the level of the law. At the moment, this has indeed been done only by a decree of the Government of the Republic.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U311	Please, Mr Eldar Efendiev!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U312	Thank you, Madam President. Honourable waitress! I will continue with the previous question and ask for clarification. Do you have information about which Member States of the European Union or outside Europe, but close to it, is it forbidden to execute animals for religious purposes or without stunning?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U313	Thank you. Given the conflicting nature of the field, a project such as Dialrel was launched in the European Union, which ended in 2010. The project collected the available scientific data on religious slaughter and the rules governing the execution of religious slaughter in different countries. Consultations with religious communities identified different religious practices and discussed the possibility of introducing scientifically proven innovations into religious killing procedures. Based on this information, I can tell you that the slaughter of animals without previous stunning is prohibited in Sweden. In essence, killing without prior stunning is also prohibited in Luxembourg, where there is a so-called gentlemen's agreement under which permits for such slaughter are not issued, even though such a possibility exists by law. Nor has the exemption been used to kill without previous stunning in Denmark or Finland. In Finland, I have to say, there are different rules. In the Åland region, killing without stunning is prohibited by law. In other parts of Finland, the law allows the animal to be slaughtered for religious purposes, provided that the stunning takes place at the same time as the cut being carried out. In Cyprus, too, the exemption provided for in the Act has not been used for the slaughter of animals without prior stunning. These were the countries of the European Union. The killing of animals without prior stunning is also prohibited in Norway and New Zealand. That would be the whole picture of all the world's powers.

I think if you try to think over or mysticize, fearing that there might be some really subtle ulterior motive, it's not going anywhere. You can't always suspect there's some kind of bilateral agreement somewhere that might call this principle into question. Estonia operates very clearly in the international community, we have been accepted into important international organisations and no one has challenged our legal continuity.

Do you still think that it is right not to support the proposal of the Centre Party, not to come to the aid of people and not to lower the electricity excise duty as early as next year?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U401	Thank you. Yes, I continue to believe that it is unreasonable to lower the electric excise duty. We remember that we introduced electric excises because European regulations require the introduction of electric excises. However, we did not impose this electricity excise duty on an empty space, but when we introduced an electricity excise, we lost the air pollution charge imposed on electricity generation. The excise duty replaced the air pollution charge. I can say that this practice has also been implemented in another Member State of the European Union. I am now reminded, for example, of Denmark, where the electricity excise tax paid by the household consumer also includes the charge for carbon dioxide emissions, i.e. the charge for air pollution, and this excise duty is as much as 24 times that of Estonia. In the Netherlands, electricity excise duty is 25.5 times higher than in Estonia. In Sweden it is 7.1 times larger, significantly larger than in Estonia. So there are very different practices. If the electricity excise tax is completely abolished in Estonia, this means that 33 million euros will not be collected in the budget. This means that either pensions must be reduced or social assistance must not be provided. That doesn't make any sense. If the excise duty is abolished, it will not be the poorest, those who need the help of the state, but those who consume the most electricity will win. I don't think that's fair.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U404	Thank you. This is, of course, the question of the choice of social assistance systems. Tax money is collected in Estonia and assistance is provided to people either financially or as a service. In some countries, such as the United Kingdom, different VAT rates are also used to provide social assistance. In some countries, different reduced commodity prices are used to help social groups in difficulty. It's a question of choice. In Estonia, the current system of income support has well justified itself. Income support does indeed reach those in need. I would argue that the effectiveness of the social assistance system is crucial in difficult circumstances. If aid is granted through different tax rates or goods are sold cheaper to certain social groups, then the effectiveness of State aid is not, in my opinion, at the level that it is when income support is paid directly to those in need.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U416	Thank you. I do not agree with the question of the construction of networks. We need to build nets. For example, water tariffs in East Germany were then four or five times higher than in West Germany, despite the fact that wages in East Germany were much lower than in West Germany. But the reason was clear to everyone. While the necessary investments were constantly made in West Germany for 50 years, they were not made in East Germany. Our transmission networks are actually in the same kind of investment shortage. What has not been done in its time must be made up for now. I do agree with you on the issue of meters. I, too, do not consider it appropriate to switch meters so frequently, even though I understand those who are involved in this exchange. The argument is, in particular, that the new meters will reduce electricity losses very strongly and ultimately the investment will be profitable.

I believe that we will find such a solution there that this suspension of herd-books, which is the result of certain shortcomings in the breeding programme, will be lifted. With regard to breeding issues now, I hope that this will remain the preserve of experts and scientists. I don't know what to say here. There was a story earlier about Peipsi onion. I'm not a specialist to appreciate that. This must remain a matter for experts and scientists. I guess that's how it's gonna be, and I guess it's gonna be settled here.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U432	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I have a draft law amending the Police and Border Guard Act on behalf of the Group of the Estonian Reform Party and the Group of the Isamaa and Res Publica Union. The point is that the various pensions of police and border guards are currently calculated on the basis of several laws. As a result, most of them can be calculated and changed by indexation, but a small part of them is amended to take account of changes in the level of pay or in the level of remuneration. Our aim is to ensure equal treatment of all these persons. We want all these pensions to be calculated and amended in the future by indexing them. We hope that the procedure for the draft will be successful, meaningful and speedy. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U433	Speaking chair of the Riigikogu, Sven Sester, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U434	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I hand over the Riigikogu's decision to appoint a member of the Supervisory Board for granting grants from the Gambling Tax. Here is the text of the decision itself and also the explanatory memorandum. A few words about it. The Committee on Monetary Affairs decided at its meeting on 19 November to initiate the draft decision of the Riigikogu on the appointment of a member of the Supervisory Board for granting grants from gambling tax. 9 November s. a the Minister of Social Affairs proposed to the Committee on Finance that the representative of the Ministry of Social Affairs, Riho Rahuoja, be removed from the Council's membership of the Gambling Tax and that Chancellor Marelle Erlenheim be appointed as the new representative of the Ministry of Social Affairs in the composition of the Council. According to § 7 (4) of the Gambling Tax Act, the Council of the Riigikogu is formed from the Gambling Tax. This council includes 6 members of the Riigikogu and 1 representative of the Ministry of Education and Research, 1 representative of the Ministry of Culture and 1 representative of the Ministry of Social Affairs. According to § 117 (1) of the Law on the Rules of Procedure and Homes of the Riigikogu, the draft decision of the Riigikogu on the appointment of a member of the Supervisory Board is discussed at a single reading. Paragraph 2 of the same section provides that if the draft has been submitted by a body whose right of proposal derives from the Constitution or Act of the Republic of Estonia, no amendments to the draft will be tabled. In other cases, the President of the Riigikogu sets a deadline for the submission of amendments in the course of the procedure. Amendments may be tabled only by political groups. The Committee on Finance decided to propose that the assembly set a deadline for the submission of amendments. a November 27, 2004. Thank you.

" He was told the bill number, but he asked what it was specifically. He was told what the title of the bill was. He started, said a few sentences, and then he said, "Oh, I don't bother! " There were officials sitting by the wall, and he said to one of them, " Well, tell them! ' That's what I'm saying! Even the minister doesn't bother! What is the difference between the debates that take place in the Commission, specifically in the Committee on Monetary Affairs, and in this Chamber? You may leave this room, you may not listen, but everything they say will be recorded. It's all left for history. We write down our opinions, our priorities, our principles, they remain. In this sense, the hall is like a river, a living water that flows, and in which it is sometimes pleasant to swim. But the Committee on Monetary Affairs is a swamp because there is nothing left of it. Look at these protocols. There is nothing to understand, because there are only figures on how the vote was taken, but there are no positions. Now explain it to me. If the minister comes to the Committee on Monetary Affairs and says that he does not want to talk in the Commission, what is the point of the opposition wasting its strength there if there is no verbatim if there is no broadcast on the Internet? I, once again, proposed last spring that it all be. In our view, the debate is welcome and it must take place in a Chamber where, however, there is a small chance that people will know what we are talking about and how we will argue our views. The debate in the Commission does not make any sense in the current situation, because even in the worst case scenario for the coalition, the vote will end at 6: 5. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U681	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, The Riigikogu has prepared the Riigikogu statement on the reform of Estonian agriculture and the common agricultural policy of the European Union. When adopting the application, the Riigikogu continues to confirm its support for Estonian agriculture and stresses the importance of rural life in this regard, in particular with regard to food supply, regional development and economic and security policy aspects. Given the geographical location and long-term traditions of our land resources, Estonian agriculture has great potential. As the representative of the drafters of the application, I would like to highlight the following. The Treaty on European Union states that the Union promotes economic, social and territorial cohesion and solidarity between Member States. We have been called on to solidarity with the countries of southern Europe in the debt crisis, and Estonia has expressed its willingness to do so despite its modest possibilities. While respecting the fundamental principles of the European Union, we have a legitimate expectation that Europe will be able to express its solidarity with Estonia through equal treatment of our farmers. A faster harmonisation of direct payments to farmers in the Baltic States would mean a redistribution of less than 1% of funds under the first pillar of the CAP. This would be a small step for the European Union, but a very important step for farmers in the Baltic countries. Ensuring solidarity costs incomparably less than losing faith in solidarity. The European Union is strong if it takes account of the specificities of the Member States. In many EU countries, more than half of the total land area is arable land, and new measures need to be planned to reduce the environmental impact of agriculture. The European Commission has proposed to create mandatory "ecological focus areas" in all Member States, which means excluding part of the arable land from use. There are currently nearly 300000 hectares of agricultural land in Estonia that has been used, but which we do not currently use. In addition, forests, swamps, bogs, as well as semi-natural communities cover about two thirds of the land. We have taken and will continue to take various measures to reduce the environmental impact of agriculture, including the protection and enhancement of biodiversity, but we cannot agree in any way with the proposal to exclude another significant part of our farmland from use. We look forward to taking account of the specificities of the Member States on this issue. As stated in the explanatory memorandum to the statement of the Riigikogu, when the Riigikogu adopts a statement in support of the agricultural sector, it is very important in the final phase of the budget talks in the next financial period. In doing so, the Riigikogu gives a strong signal to the European Commission, the European Parliament and the Member States about the importance we attach to competitive and sustainable agriculture in the European Union and Estonia. It is also a message to farmers, rural residents and the Estonian agricultural sector in general, reporting that agriculture is seen as an important part of the development of the Estonian economy and of rural areas. In our statement, we call on the Member States of the European Union, the European Parliament and the European Commission to: (1) introduce a system of CAP direct payments which would ensure a level playing field for all farmers in the European Union; (2) move to a new methodology for calculating direct payments in the next financial period of the European Union, which would avoid significant differences between the highest and lowest levels of direct payments in the European Union and take account of economic and environmental criteria; (3) continue to grant direct payments and finance rural development measures in such a way as to ensure the sustainable development of agriculture and rural areas; (4) take a flexible approach, taking into account the specificities of the Member States, as a condition for additional payments for the implementation of environmentally sound agricultural practices, to the proposed 7% ecological focus area requirement; (5) give Member States implementing the single area payment system the possibility to include this land suitable for agricultural production which was not in good agricultural order as at 30 June 2003 and for which it has not been possible to apply for a single area payment so far. Finally, I would like to thank all the members of the Riigikogu, the officials of the Committee on Rural Affairs and the officials of the Ministry of Agriculture and the Minister of Agriculture, who, with their proposals, have made a very important and important contribution to the text of our statement. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U740	Good Chairman of the Riigikogu! Good fellows! I am talking about the things that we have been talking about in the discussions in the Commission, not only in the last few weeks, but also in the past. In fact, ladies and gentlemen, do you remember what happened here exactly a year ago? Who performed here on the remote exactly a year ago, on 21 November 2011? That was Jerzy Buzek, President of the European Parliament at the time. What did he tell us here? In his speech, he said, among other things: "You have some doubts as to whether this policy (He had in mind the common agricultural policy). (K.K.) is fair enough. We are facing a reform of the common agricultural policy and we do not know how this debate will end. I understand very well the hopes of you and your citizens. ' That is what the President of the European Parliament said a year ago on this very platform. By the time the President spoke on this board, the Commission had already proposed a reform of the common agricultural policy. We know of this Commission proposal, which was presented on 12 October 2011, that the level of direct payments to Estonia is 156 euros, or 58% of the European average. We knew the overall objective that the European Commission had in order to bring direct payments to 90% of the European average, but the time it took did not satisfy us at all. The goal was noble, but we were not satisfied for too long to reach the end result. Since then, this issue has been in various formats in this Chamber. This has been discussed in briefings and answers to questions. On 25 November, the Baltic Assembly adopted a resolution calling on the Member States of the European Union, the European Parliament and the European Commission to harmonise direct payments for agriculture in such a way that there are no significant differences between the levels of support and to move from 1 January 2014 to new methods for calculating direct payments and to continue supporting agriculture and rural areas in order to ensure sustainable development of agriculture and rural areas in the European Union. It was the Baltic Assembly. On 12 April, a joint meeting of the Committee on Rural Affairs of the Estonian, Latvian and Lithuanian Parliament took place right here in the building of the Riigikogu, during which the chairmen of these committees signed a joint declaration expressing dissatisfaction with the pace of harmonisation of direct payments proposed by the European Commission. This declaration was also forwarded to national parliaments, the European Parliament and the European Commission. Less than a month after sending out this declaration, Mr Ciolos, the European Commissioner for Agriculture and Rural Development, also responded, in a few sentences, that he understood our concerns and that he would do everything in his power to change this situation. There has been action within the walls of this house as well. Namely, many of you signed a joint declaration at the premises of the Rural Affairs Committee when the Estonian Chamber of Agriculture and Trade and the Estonian Farmers' Confederation, together with Latvian and Lithuanian colleagues, collected signatures for the joint declaration in June this year to support the harmonisation of direct payments during the next budget period of the European Union. 25,000 signatures were collected. We may think that we have really done so much, that we have already done everything. Then why the joint statement again? But we know that tomorrow and the day after tomorrow there will be a meeting of the Heads of Government in Brussels to discuss the draft budget for the years 2014 to 2020. I dare say that it is at this meeting that our agricultural subsidies will be fixed. The latest developments, as far as I know, show that our issue of unequal subsidies has not been included in the budget plan's negotiating plan. It's a sign of serious danger. This is not included in the amendments. On the table is the paper that Mr Rompuy brought before the heads of government, which once again has 58% inside. Although we had a little glimpse of hope. Indeed, the report by the European Parliament's Committee on Agriculture and Rural Development on this Council Regulation establishing rules for direct payments to farmers under support schemes within the framework of the common agricultural policy. The rapporteur was Mr Santos. In this report we can read that the minimum number of direct payments that a Member State can receive is 65% of the average level. It was a very large beam of light for us. But now, once again, there are papers on the heads of government's desk, which says 58%. Consequently, I believe that we need to make both today's statement and more action and statements that the grant should be distributed more jointly and severally. We do not agree with what the European Commission is offering us. If you now come to the text of the statement by the Riigikogu, I will mention some of the major issues that have often been the subject of debate in the Committee on Rural Affairs over this year, even two years. I dare say that every time we discuss future perspectives, these issues are on the table. One issue really is the inequality of direct payments, but that is what I have already talked about. The second point is greening. Good friends! The European Commission has proposed that 7% of arable land currently in active use should be excluded from production during greening. Let me say a few numbers here. Do you guys know that 52% of the area of Estonia is under the forest, i.e. 52% of our homeland is under the forest? 18% are nature reserves, plus marine nature reserves. This 18% is on land. 7% are lakes. The rest is everything else. Only 20% of our land is arable land. Where do we get the 7% here? There is one point in our statement: Member States must be given flexibility so that they can decide for themselves the greening areas. In Estonia, where a small field ends with forest, ditch, brush, pile of stone or something like that, it is not practical if we take 7% of this already small or limited resource out of active agricultural production. For that we have swamps, nature reserves, forests. Another important nuance: we need to worry not only about the size of direct payments. The 'envelope' of the whole agricultural policy, the second axis, or support for the rural development plan, is important to us. We certainly must not make concessions to increase direct payments at the expense of the rural development plan, the second axis. These grants are primarily intended for those areas that were asked about in this Chamber. We have few small productions left; the number of people employed in the agricultural sector remains small. It is these second-axis subsidies that we are reviving life on land as a whole, including support for agriculture. Support for the second axis is intended both now and probably during the next financial perspective, as we can read from the European Commission's papers, precisely to support small producers. Now to the decisions of the Committee on Rural Affairs. Following the adoption of the draft procedure, the Rural Commission discussed the statement 'Estonian agriculture and the reform of the European Union's common agricultural policy' at 306 its two meetings: s. a on 5 and 12 November. It must be said that the Commission also took part in the preparation of the draft statement, discussing this statement already before it was put into procedure on 22 October. One might say that it was at this meeting that this draft statement was very much formed. At this meeting, proposals from all the political groups were on the table, as the Committee on Rural Affairs has members from all the political groups. At the meeting on 5 November, it was decided to appoint Kalvi Tõud, Chairman of the Committee on Rural Affairs, as well as myself, to send the draft to the Riigikogu for reading on 15 November 2012, but I could only come to this Chamber to report this draft on 21 November. The Bureau had set the deadline of 8 November at 6 p.m. for the submission of amendments when the draft had been adopted. By this time no amendments to the draft were received from the political groups of the Riigikogu. At its meeting on 12 November, a number of points in the application were discussed again and the linguistic amendments made by the language editor and the harmonisation of the style of the application were reviewed. The committee decided to submit the linguistically adjusted text of the draft to the Riigikogu plenary session for the first reading. According to § 154 (7) of the Riigikogu Rules of Procedure and Homes Act, the final vote on the draft statement of the Riigikogu is taken at first reading. As the lead committee, the Rural Commission proposes to adopt the draft as a statement. It should be said that the adoption of the draft requires a majority of votes in favour. Thank you.

But we are in the big market, we are in Russia that is not part of the European Union, China is farther away. The single European agricultural market is also affected by this price. So there's a lot of discussion and question points here. In any case, we must make sure that the common European instruments do not decrease and that the instruments that come to implement the common agricultural policy are certainly not reduced.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U790	Mr President of the Riigikogu. Ladies and gentlemen, The Reform Party Group supports the Riigikogu statement on agriculture in Estonia and the reform of the European Union's common agricultural policy. This supports the existence of domestic food commodities and the sustainability of rural areas. The CAP is one of the most central policies of the European Union, covering 43% of the EU budget, i.e. EUR 53 billion per year, of which the direct payments represent 47%. It is an old and rigid system that has gradually been tried to reform. Any support is distortion of the market situation and protectionism. There is an increase in bureaucracy, while the agricultural support package is very mixed. According to the fundamental principles of the European Union, we attach great importance to equal conditions of competition in the common economic space, taking into account climate and environmental conditions. However, based on real policy, the solution for the next financial period will be to increase direct payments for Estonian farmers, not to harmonise our support by reducing direct payments from other countries. However, the overall volume of direct payments under the common agricultural policy should not be increased. The equality of direct payments must not be at the expense of rural development either. The current Estonia-specific NAP of 50% for the first pillar as direct payments and 50% for the second pillar as rural development should remain valid. The current average level of direct payments in the European Union is EUR 280 per hectare. We support the direction of the European Commission to ensure that the level of direct payments in any Member State will not be below 90% of the average in the future. The minimum that could be accepted in the coming financial period is the equalisation of all direct payments from the Baltic States with direct payments from Romania, i.e. 75% of the European Union average, or EUR 210 per hectare. In this case too, this would be the lowest level in the European Union. The price of equality would be only 0.07% of the CAP budget. It is only a matter of will and real solidarity. Estonia's GDP per capita and agricultural subsidies are correlated. We should not accept that the European Union does not appreciate the Estonian economy and considers our prospects for development to be modest. Estonia's future must not be a land of easy and cheap work. In the long term, first of all, we consider it important to have budgetary flexibility, i.e. a gradual reduction in ownership direct payments and continued support for investments, rural enterprises and rural areas. In other words, we prefer rural measures, i.e. the line to direct payments, i.e. fish. Secondly, we consider it important that R &amp; D and innovation be contributed much more. Thirdly, the system of direct payments should be simplified and made more effective. Fourthly, market management measures should be developed. Fifthly, agricultural land actually used should be eligible for support, active farmers should be supported and the requirement of a 7% eco-zone should be treated flexibly. More time, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U791	Three minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U792	Although the common agricultural policy is not the answer to all rural problems, agriculture remains a key factor in rural life. The agricultural sector is certainly one of the country's security risks in terms of securing the security of domestic food supply for its inhabitants. Product development and the competitiveness of our farmers are directly linked to the investments being made and depend on the level of support. Bioenergy and water management still have a major development perspective. Valuable jobs will be added. It is an effective tool against urbanisation. The problem of aftergrowth in agriculture will be alleviated. It is worth noting that the creation of greater added value for agricultural products and the production of agricultural products imported to date on their own land are an important counterbalance to the country's external trade balance. Necessary investment in support systems, such as land improvement and roads and bridges, is also essential, sometimes with only two to four years of payback. Large producers will survive under unequal conditions of competition, but modernisation of agriculture will lead to job losses. If we expect greater solidarity from the European Union, we must also have solidarity in our own country. Farmers who have received less support so far deserve more equal treatment. All countries in the world support their agriculture, though in very different ways. Whether the trade negotiations between the European Union and the United States, which supports significantly more farmers, will lead to fundamental changes and whether we will continue with domestic, relatively softer and more expensive food, or whether GM food will be our new challenge, is demonstrated by time. We thank all the authors of the statement. ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U804	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! Domestic agriculture is a great asset. In Estonia, agriculture has been an important industry throughout the ages, the designer of our landscape and the guarantor of the people's survival in difficult times. The farmer's greatest wealth is the land. 'The agricultural land area has steadily increased in recent years,' was already written in 1939 in the statistical yearbook "Estonian Agriculture', but we can say the same for the last few years. In 2004, direct payments to 818000 hectares of agricultural land were applied for, compared to 911000 hectares in 2012. We have more potential than that. The increase in milk production and productivity is faster after regaining independence, positive developments are taking place in the meat sector and in cereal farming. The reasons for such developments are better technology, the growing awareness of farmers, the slowly evolving joint action, but certainly also the European Union's subsidies. In 2004, a total of 44.3 million euros of direct payments and additional direct payments were paid in Estonia, but 116.4 million euros last year. Agriculture is more than any other industry affected by a number of natural and man-made risks. The depletion of water and soil resources, the increasing number of droughts and floods and the constant growth of the world's population have a direct impact on Estonia as well. The rise in the prices of cereals on the world market as a result of droughts this year quickly reached Estonia as well. This shows that the existence of domestic agriculture is invaluable in order to guarantee food security. Competitive agriculture is the best guarantee of food security. This requires a level playing field. The final version of the negotiating document for the new financial period of the European Union states: 'In the case of a more equal distribution of direct payments between Member States, account shall be taken of the still existing differences in wage levels, purchasing power, output in the agricultural sector and input prices. ' It is unclear how these indicators are taken into account and how it is possible that, given them, direct payments in countries with similar levels of wages and input prices differ so much. Lower wages are also the consequence of lower direct payments. Empowering income and standard of living differences with different direct payments cannot be an objective of the common agricultural policy. Estonia does not want more subsidies, but more equal opportunities in the European Union's internal market. However, Estonia's land relations after the land reform and the large share of farmers' leases remain a serious problem also after the equality of direct payments. It is also necessary to find the objectives of today's circumstances and the content supporting sustainable agriculture on the outdated foundations of direct payments. It is necessary to clarify whether decoupled direct payments are aimed at supporting landowners, compensating farmers for loss of income, greener agriculture, a more stable safety net, creating a public good, combating climate change, socio-economic support for family farms, increasing the investment capacity of large producers, lower consumer prices, creating advantages for current farmers through payment entitlements, or all of these objectives at once. If we do not find answers to these questions, it would be more appropriate to abolish direct payments. Under unequal conditions, the cost of the survival of Estonian agriculture has been lower wages, more work, rapid concentration of production and industrial change, which have led to the difficult situation of family farms. In addition to efficient large-scale production, we also need small-scale farming farms that preserve land settlements and landscapes and diversify agricultural production. According to the latest agricultural census, there were 19613 agricultural holdings in Estonia in 2010, 5% of which accounted for three quarters of the total agricultural production in the country. Estonian farmers must be acknowledged that they are able to develop and even compete with the best in Europe. Estonian food supply and export production and the introduction of the most modern technology will be largely borne by large producers in the near future. But the development of Estonia, where small ones become bigger or stop and large ones buy foreign capital, does not meet our national interests. The more we are masters-owners in our own country, the greater the prerequisites for successful rural development. Agriculture is still the most national economic sector in Estonia. In implementing the common agricultural policy and the rural development plan for the next budgetary period, we must ask ourselves: firstly, where there is a balance between ever more industrialised and economically efficient large production and traditional family farms, secondly, how can food production and the more targeted use of land resources be maintained as an agricultural policy objective? These are our most important national agricultural policy decisions. Today, we are not talking about the need to support our agriculture, not because it is an underdeveloped and uncertain economic area with no vision for the future. On the contrary, agriculture in Estonia today is a highly innovative, rapidly developing export-able area. As shown by the study of growth areas under the Development Fund, agriculture is one of the so-called "growing areas" around the world. The Riigikogu has prepared a statement that is a message of symbolic significance to Brussels and other Member States. The European Union's common agricultural policy needs changes and must embrace the enlargement of the European Union in 2004. The two-speed common agricultural policy does not strengthen the unity of the European Union or the competitiveness of agriculture. I would like to thank the members of the Riigikogu for the initiative, attention and support you give to Estonian agriculture with today's statement! This is a recognition of the progress made by the agricultural sector in recent years, but also a message to the Estonian public that agriculture has become one of the priorities of the Estonian state. I hope that, in cooperation with the Riigikogu, the Government of the Republic and the representative organisations of farmers, we will achieve a common agricultural policy that is fairer for Estonia and more useful for the European Union as a whole. Thank you very much.

But because the rate of excise duty increased a few months ago, prices increased and tourists immediately reacted. The occupancy of hotels and everything related to tourism fell for a while. And it was a marked decline. Later, things stabilized, of course, and life moved on. But why did I ask that question? Because such effects need to be taken into account. All sectors that are directly or indirectly linked to the rise of excise duties must plan ahead that such times will come when they will inevitably have to lose their turnover. After all, the increase in alcohol prices is also a punishment for alcohol consumers, but this penalty is not a high annual increase of 5% in excise duty over four years between 2013 and 2016. This punishment can be assessed as quite flexible, and the initiators of the law also showed themselves to be good. The step-by-step increase in excise duties is very sensible, and I also support it. However, since our debate has been broad today and many other issues have also been addressed, whether or not they relate to excise duties, I would like to highlight the increase in other prices. I can't just leave a few words to touch another sales item, which is also getting more expensive. It's electricity. I do not understand why alcohol can be made more expensive in stages and flexibly, but not electricity. It is understandable, however, that alcohol consumption is a matter of choice, while electricity consumption is a matter of life. Of course, you can say that electricity is also a matter of choice, you can choose life without electricity. But it probably doesn't take any effort to imagine what life is without electricity. The current level of electricity excise duty is a punishment for our poorest people in particular. Alcohol excise is growing gradually and, as I said, it is very right. But since electric excises are rising exponentially, I don't think that's right. The basis could be the European Union's minimum level and a step-up approach. Why do we have to punish our people and municipalities, who, even after the surge in electricity prices, have to maintain infrastructure, such as kindergartens, schools, cultural centres, social welfare institutions, etc., and collide costs in other areas, which means reducing wages and formations? This isn't right. But I support this bill! Thank you.
