ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U945	According to studies carried out in OECD countries, alcohol consumption is 16% lower in countries with limited alcohol advertising. In countries where restrictions on soft alcohol advertising apply in addition to strong alcohol, the amount of alcohol consumed is still 11% lower. So banning the promotion of both strong and lean alcohol means a total of nearly a third less alcohol consumption. The number of road accidents in countries with strong alcohol advertising restrictions has decreased by 10% and in countries with both strong and lean alcohol advertising restrictions by 23%. The link between drinking and advertising among young people has also been convincingly demonstrated by World Health Organisation studies. Five minutes of watching alcohol advertising increases alcohol consumption by a bottle of lean beer a day. The effect of alcohol advertising on young people is also extremely long lasting. People who saw more alcohol advertising on young TV used to consume more alcohol than others throughout their subsequent lives, and the importance of each ad they saw clearly emerged. Each advertisement viewed increased the volume of drinks consumed by 1%. The biggest problem with these ads is that alcohol consumption is served as safe, risk-free and problem-free activities, with only positive emotions. However, potential health risks and other negative consequences will be overlooked. Good advertising also reduces the impact of prevention and health education. At the same time, there have been a number of signals in Estonia about the willingness to limit alcohol advertising, including from alcohol producers and importers themselves. This is understandable, because many other European countries have gone down the road of banning advertising. For example, all alcohol advertising has been banned in Norway, as did our neighbour Lithuania last year. In Finland, there is a ban on the advertising of strong alcohol; in Sweden, advertising of alcohol is completely prohibited on radio and outdoor media, on television in France. Just as Estonia has remained stable at the top of many black rankings since regaining independence, however, we are also at the top of Europe in terms of alcohol deaths for men and women. Such figures are frightening in our small country and, taking into account the data of the Conjuncture Institute, we can claim that alcohol consumption is self-destructive to the gene pool of the Estonian people. In addressing the Estonian Association of Family Doctors, the Estonian Health Care Workers' Trade Union and the Estonian Sisters' Association to the Riigikogu, attention has been drawn to the urgent need for additional funding of health care. Alcohol and tobacco excise duties also play an important role in the implementation of the state budget. In the case of a reasonable level of alcohol duty, excise duty would limit excessive drinking and smoking. If the amounts from alcohol duty were earmarked for health care, the consequences of smoking and overdrinking could be further mitigated. Directing excise duties to medical treatment and raising the wages of health care workers would enable the Estonian medical system to organise treatment activities more efficiently and more specifically. The Central Party Group has proposed to introduce a legislative amendment that would direct 20% of excise duties on alcohol and tobacco into the funds of the Health Insurance Fund. This would be one of the keys to reducing health harm caused by the consumption of alcoholic beverages. It is not possible to solve this problem by merely increasing excise duty. At the same time, we must not forget the activation of the shadow economy in the excessive roll-up of excise duty. One of the most tragic examples is the so-called methanol tragedy of Pärnu, which resulted in 68 deaths. In the days it took place, the secret vodka was considerably cheaper than the grocery vodka. It would be wrong to think that in time, the problem of bootlegged vodka has been reduced. For example, last year, the Director of the Conjuncture Institute said that when a person discovers that the price has risen sharply for him, a large proportion of consumers do not stop buying alcohol but try to find other alternative sources, such as the secret market. The Director of the Institute of Economic Research pointed out that while in 2007 the share of illegal alcohol was only 10% of the wine market in peak times of the economy, in 2010, for example, it accounted for a quarter of the wine market. The study also showed that 9% of respondents had purchased alcohol on the black market in 2010, compared to only around 3% in 2007. Thus, any increase in alcohol duty for as noble a purpose as it may be, must be made to ensure that the opposite consequences are not achieved. Rather, if we want to limit our drinking, we can make people get firewater from places beyond the corner, which may mean risking damage to health or even death. Various views have remained on the issue of increasing excise duty. For example, a doctoral thesis defended in 2011 at the Faculty of Economics of the University of Tartu found that in order to improve the health of the Estonian population and tax receipts, excise duties on alcohol should be increased by 50 to 200%. It is suggested that the introduction of a 50% higher excise duty would save a million euros of health expenditure per year in the long term and bring 50 million euros of tax revenue, but in order to obtain tax revenue, the alcohol taxation policy in Estonia should be based on more thorough analyses than has been the case so far. At the same time, when raising the tax, the Minister of Finance does not have to pay attention to us and talk only about surprising goals, such as reducing overdrinking and smoking, etc. We know that, in the case of a government as cynical as we currently have, the objective of the tax increase can be primarily to increase the revenue of the state budget in order to pay off, for example, the Greek party or the tax cuts that favour Estonia's wealthy. Thank you.

However, the truth is that many people in Estonia still have the opportunity to deal with only one hobby, which to a large extent is nothing more than drinking. I also think that the use of the money collected by raising the tax should be carefully considered where to place it. When it comes to solving a drinking problem, it should be done in a complex way. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U975	Thank you very much. Please, colleague Mailis Reps! Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U976	Honourable Chairwoman of the Riigikogu! Happy members of the Riigikogu in the hall and in front of televisions! We are currently discussing draft law 268, which bears the name of the draft law amending the Law on excise duties on alcohol, tobacco, fuel and electricity. If we look at excise duties on alcohol in the context of this draft, the question arises, whose opinion should be relied on even more than that of Lauri Beekmann, the head of the Community Non-Alcoholic Estonia. He has stressed that 2.5 million people die a year because of alcohol. In his article, Lauri Beekmann builds on the UN report on non-communicable diseases in 2011, according to which alcohol consumption is one of the four main risk factors. Other factors are unhealthy food, low mobility and tobacco. These four risk factors are responsible for 63% of the world's deaths. Alcohol manufacturers say that they produce these legal drinks as long as there is enough demand and confirm that they only promote cultural drinking. However, the fact is that 2.5 million people die a year from alcohol in the world. In other words, according to WHO, alcohol is a greater cause of death than, for example, AIDS, tuberculosis or violence. In order for producers' profits to be preserved, they must constantly find new ones in place of dying customers. Thus, drinking traditions must be maintained in different cultures and societies, so that young people who grow up start drinking as parents do. To this end, a massive marketing machine is working to promote alcohol consumption and to instill the understanding that this neurotoxic cancer addict is an integral and normal part of everyday life. At the same time, it is known that alcohol is a pleasure in every society that consumes it. In addition to these deaths, alcohol causes an almost innumerable number of various losses, ranging from vandalism to the need for special social care. Unfortunately, we are all jointly paying for all of this, or in fact we are not able to cover all of these costs. Do we dare answer the question of how good it is to protect and care for children who have been neglected by alcoholic parents in our country? What can we offer alcohol - addicted pregnant women who can still fear for the health of their unborn child and come to ask for help? How are addicts treated and rehabilitated? These questions concern part of the serious problems related to alcohol, which our country simply does not have enough money to deal with or to deal with to the extent necessary. These are budget gaps that need to be filled, and, of course, the first and most direct source for this is alcohol excise duty. Let us imagine for a moment what the price of alcohol should be if we stop paying off this social damage and put all this expense on the price of alcohol. Absurd? But the role of alcohol and the alcohol industry in our lives and society is just as absurd. We have begun to view as normal something that is obviously not normal. Producers point to the risk of an illegal market for each price increase. This threat is real and must be addressed by the state and the authorities. However, legal alcohol producers should not try to give the impression that there is a legal and underground market like night and day, two completely opposite phenomena: one tax-paying and job-creating, responsible industry, the other criminal business. Yes, of course, dealing with secret alcohol is criminal, but let us not forget that the main cause of the problem of alcohol throughout society is the impact of these drinks on people. After all, it does not matter whether the alcohol is legally produced in Russia and brought to Estonia secretly or produced legally in Estonia, the effect is one. We cannot talk about health or safety for any alcoholic beverage. The illegal market, a small part of which is particularly dangerous and without any control of the liquids prepared in the cellars, requires vigorous intervention, but in doing so, the business promised by the state cannot be set aside for some special pedestal. Illegal and legal business is actually intertwined in its own way and represent two sides of one coin. In our society, there is legal industry for the promotion and advertising of alcohol, and thus also for the shaping of drinkers from a growing generation. With their advertising, product design and a huge sales network, they are attracting new customers. When some of these people experience serious difficulties in life, becoming unemployed and losing their social status, they start looking for cheaper alcohol underground. The secret market feeds on the fruits of the legal market. At the same time, tax-paying producers use the secret market as a trump card to combat measures to reduce their business income. September 9th is the world day of fetal alcohol syndrome. The symbolic ninth day of the month reminds us that nine months of pregnancy a woman should give up alcohol completely on behalf of her unborn child. Unfortunately, not everyone can or is unaware of it, and therefore, about one child in 100 is born with a birth defect caused by alcohol. Its most severe form, FASD, is estimated to be present in one to three children per thousand births, according to conservative estimates. Of course, these figures are influenced by the quantities of alcohol consumed by the individual people and, overall, by the scale of the problem. Based on these statistics, it would be expected that there will be 15/45 FASD-diagnosed children in Estonia a year and about 150 children with alcohol-induced birth defects. In this light, it should not be forgotten that Estonia is at the forefront of the world's countries in terms of alcohol consumption, and that is why we must pay particular attention to the 268 SE procedures. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U977	Thank you very much. Now, please, colleague Jüri Ratas!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U978	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Good fellows! Overconsumption of alcohol is a serious and very worrying problem, directly or indirectly suffered by all people. Alcohol consumption causes many illnesses and health problems, including heart disease, accidents, injuries, and mental behavioral disorders. On average, over 10 litres of pure alcohol was consumed per resident in Estonia last year, which is 5.7% more than the year before. This is equal to a bottle of 51 half-litres of vodka or 408 bottles of beer with a strength of 5%. In reality, this figure is even misleadingly low, because the amount of alcohol that has been drunk is divided by the number of all Estonian residents, including adults with abstinence, for example. According to Emor, 70 to 90% of the market volume is consumed by weekly drinkers, who account for 10 to 25% of the adult population. According to a study carried out by the Estonian Institute of Conjuncture, 75% of people agree that we have too much to drink, but only 2% thought that about themselves. 56% of those surveyed considered their alcohol consumption to be low, 29% to be moderate, and 13% confirmed that they do not drink alcohol at all. If the result of this study were to be realistic, we would live in almost a perfect country. Unfortunately, when looking at the quantities of tarbitur, it is clear that real life is unfortunately not so rosy. The most common alcoholic beverage is, as we know, beer. This iodine was converted into pure alcohol last year by 4.1 litres, or 164 bottles of 5 per cent volts of beer per person. Strong alcohol, calculated in absolute alcohol, is also popular: 3.7 litres per capita. World Health Organisation experts estimate that 6 litres of pure alcohol per capita is a critical limit, with a higher amount causing significant harm to public health. In the worst times in Estonia, this norm has been twice as much, and last year 70% more was consumed than was considered dangerous. This is not a pseudo-problem, therefore, but a serious challenge for the Estonian state and the Estonian people as a whole. Under the pressure of the non-profit association Alcohol-free Estonia and also the Centre Party, a ban on the sale of alcohol at night was introduced in the Riigikogu since 14 July 2008, which has certainly contributed to reducing alcohol consumption. However, it is clear that a time limit alone will not solve this problem. The desired result has also not been achieved by the quite liberal advertising restrictions in force. Alcohol advertising is prohibited in advertising law from 7 a.m. until 9 a.m. At the same time, according to Emor, minors watch most television between 19.30 and 23:00, which means that alcohol ads reach them as well. Dear Chairman, may I ask for more time?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U979	Sure, there's always time for alcohol. Three minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-11-21_U980	Thank you. The Central Party of the Riigikogu therefore supports the total ban on alcohol advertising and submitted a bill to this effect last year. Our aim is to ensure, above all, that children and young people are protected against this advertising by stricter restrictions on alcohol advertising and to reduce the consumption of alcohol in general. I think it is particularly important to focus on minors because, despite the current law, drinking is a popular form of entertainment for people under 18 years of age. According to the Centre Party's proposal, a total ban on alcohol advertising would be imposed on television and radio as well as on outdoor advertising. The impact of advertising on consumers, especially young people and children, is very significant, as it serves alcohol consumption as a well-accepted and normal activity in society. No advertisement focuses on the many negative effects of alcohol; drinking is shown as a pleasant and safe pastime. But it's not safe. Alcohol advertising masks a wide range of health risks and negative consequences and also reduces the impact of prevention and health education. Limiting alcohol advertising is an internationally recognised measure in a complex alcohol policy. The current situation in Estonia requires new and certainly more effective solutions. We can mention Norway, where all alcohol advertising has been banned, including in print media, radio, television, as well as outdoor advertising. And in Norway 35% less alcohol is consumed than in Estonia. The links between youth alcohol consumption and advertising have also been convincingly proven. Five minutes of watching alcohol advertising greatly increases the consumption of liquor. The effects of alcohol advertising on young people are also extremely long lasting. I am therefore in the same position as the head of the Institute of Economics: the price of alcoholic beverages must rise faster than for other goods, not at the same pace as the draft proposed by the Government of the Republic provides. Of course, price increases must not be so high as to increase the share of the secret market. Thank you.
